Turkey to the chairman of the Grand National Assembly There are serious structural obstacles to the economic efficiency of public tenders, the quality of the job, the benefit of the community, the respect of the environment, the transparency of the control mechanisms, the translucent and the fair. Public tender Act 4734 and Public Contracts Act 4735 have changed over a hundred times in recent years, and these law-abiding regulations have changed over a hundred in recent decades. Public tenders, however, have a structure far from meeting public needs, especially inefficient, expensive, unfinished jobs in the construction site. While high-level firms can't accept tenders, with a reputation for commitments, finishing jobs and experience, some firms are getting tenders from unrealistic prices, and as a result, things are incomplete. It's been the industry's routine to bid on far below realistic numbers. Many jobs are being tendered, knowing that they cannot be completed, as a result, the state and the nation suffer. In this area, the views of all sectors must be taken. Public tender legislation must be subjected to a complete change by receiving opinions from vocational rooms and troops and experts in public institutions. The amendments to Public Contracts Act 4734 and the Law on Public Contracts 4735 appear to be a framework of many issues, but difficulties from secondary legislation require total and detailed assessment of this area. In order to evaluate the legislation to suit the purpose of public tenders, we demand that Parliament's investigation be opened under Articles 98 of the Constitution, 104 and 105 of the Civil Code. tbmm-2016-07-12sit04spe0455par0370	Thank you. Mr. President, dear lawmakers, before I begin my words, I offer my greetings and regards to the General Assembly. Dear MPs, stone coal was found in Neyren Creek of the Black Sea on 8 November 1829 by Mehmet Long. From that day forward, thousands of mine workers have tried to remove the coal of stone, which is essential to heavy industry, day and night. During the Ottoman era, British, French, Russian companies produced stone coal, thanks to the privileges obtained by the Ottoman administration. With the formation of the Republic, Mustafa Kemal Ataturk declared Zonguldak, on April 1, 1924, the first province of the Republic, and nationalised the mines of stone coal. Great Leader Mustafa Kemal Ataturk spoke of his importance and value to Zonguldak (Zonguldak) with his saying: "The more precious the wealth under the deep lands of Zonguldak (Zonguldak), the more precious the Zonguldak is to us, the more precious the province is to us. Dear lawmakers, Zonguldak is the first province of Turkey to have carried the Turkish heavy industry on its back for years. In 1939, the Blackbuke Iron Steel Factory, the Tarmice Termic Plant in 1956, and the Ereglı Iron Steel Factory in 1965 carried heavy Turkish industry on its back for many years, starting production. The main entrance to all these factories is stone coal. Dear lawmakers, underground coal mining is the world's most heavy and dangerous task force. That's why it's a business unit where there's the most work accidents and mass killings. Many major mining accidents have occurred in Zonguldak. In Zonguldak, tens of thousands of miners were injured to extract the coal of stone, tens of thousands of miners were killed due to occupational illness, and nearly 5,000 were killed. Hence, the wives, children, parents, and brothers of these miners were victims. Here, Zonguldak, from the founding of the republic to this day, the country has been carrying its heavy industry on its back, while the Zonguldak people have paid heavy prices. Dear MPs, Law No. 6552, known in public as the Law of Torba, was published in the Official Gazette on 11 September 2014. By this law, the relatives of those who lost their lives in Soma's accident were given important rights. One of them is the right to be employed in public, as one of the spouses or children of the people who died in the mine accident in Soma. We, of course, feel that these rights should be given, which are extremely human, extremely conscientious. The law is very true in this respect, but it is also very wrong that the miners who lost their lives in Zonguldak, and Turkey, were not able to benefit from the loss of their relatives in the mining accident, and, more than that, it was contrary to the principle of equality of the Constitution. The law, in its current form, victimizes about 700 Zonguldable families.
